A free boundary problem for an immersed filament in 3D Stokes flow
Laurel Ohm

Abstract
. We consider a simplified extensible version of a dynamic free boundary problem for a thin filament with radius ϵ > 0 \epsilon>0 immersed in 3D Stokes flow. The 3D fluid is coupled to the quasi-1D filament dynamics via a novel type of angle-averaged Neumann-to-Dirichlet operator for the Stokes equations, and much of the difficulty in the analysis lies in understanding this operator. Here we show that the main part of this angle-averaged NtD map about a closed, curved filament is the corresponding operator about a straight filament, for which we can derive an explicit symbol. Remainder terms due to curvature are lower order with respect to regularity or size in ϵ \epsilon . Using this operator decomposition, it is then possible to show that the simplified free boundary evolution is a third-order parabolic equation and is locally well posed. This establishes a more complete mathematical foundation for the myriad computational results based on slender body approximations for thin immersed elastic structures.



1.
Introduction

Thin elastic filaments immersed in very viscous fluids play a central role in many biological processes, including mitotic spindle positioning during cell division
[
55
]
, cytoskeletal mechanics
[
44
]
, sperm motility
[
32
,
11
]
, and ciliary transport
[
13
]
.
To capture the effective interaction between a thin semiflexible filament and the surrounding viscous fluid, it is natural to seek a lower dimensional representation of immersed filament dynamics. This is the basic tenet of slender body theories, which play an important role in many computational models.

Given an immersed filament with finite radius
\epsilon>0
and centerline position
\bm{X}(s,t):\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
, the fundamental aim is to capture the filament evolution using an equation of the form

(1)

\frac{\partial\bm{X}}{\partial t}=-\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}(\bm{X})\big[\partial_{s}^{4}\bm{X}-\partial_{s}(\tau\partial_{s}\bm{X})\big]\,,\quad\left\lvert\partial_{s}\bm{X}\right\rvert=1\,.

Here
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}(\bm{X})
is an operator mapping force data defined along a 1D curve to a 3D velocity field governing the position of the filament centerline. This operator must also incorporate the effects of the surrounding 3D fluid on the filament evolution. The forcing terms
-\partial_{s}^{4}\bm{X}+\partial_{s}(\tau\partial_{s}\bm{X})
come from Euler-Bernoulli beam theory and model a simple elastic response along the filament, with
\tau(s,t)
, the filament tension, serving as a Lagrange multiplier to enforce the inextensibility constraint
\left\lvert\partial_{s}\bm{X}\right\rvert=1
. The key feature of this type of formulation (
1
) is that the filament dynamics are 1D. A major difficulty is coupling these 1D dynamics and the surrounding 3D fluid via the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
in both a physically and a mathematically reasonable way.

A simple and mathematically reasonable choice for this coupling operator is
local slender body theory
,
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon,{\rm loc}}^{\rm SB}(\bm{X})=c\left\lvert\log\epsilon\right\rvert({\bf I}+\partial_{s}\bm{X}\otimes\partial_{s}\bm{X})

[
31
,
18
,
6
,
32
,
70
,
10
,
11
]
. For this very simple choice of coupling, the analogue of (
1
) is a fourth order parabolic equation for the filament position coupled with a 1D elliptic equation for the filament tension
\tau
. In addition to establishing well-posedness in this setting, the author and Mori
[
51
,
58
]
prove that the filament can generate net forward motion (i.e. swim) with a suitable choice of time-periodic forcing. However,
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon,{\rm loc}}^{\rm SB}
incorporates only a very simplified description of the surrounding fluid physics on the filament evolution.

To better capture the physics of the fluid-structure interaction, a natural attempt would be to use
nonlocal
slender body theory
\mathcal{L}^{\rm SB}_{\epsilon,{\rm nloc}}(\bm{X})

[
36
,
34
,
69
]
, which, in addition to the terms from
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon,{\rm loc}}^{\rm SB}
, includes a 1D integral operator incorporating nonlocal interactions along the filament. This is a popular choice for computational models
[
41
,
64
,
68
,
40
,
17
,
4
,
60
,
43
,
45
]
because it is more physically realistic than
\mathcal{L}^{\rm SB}_{\epsilon,{\rm loc}}
and the evolution (
1
) is still relatively straightforward to compute numerically. However, the classical
\mathcal{L}^{\rm SB}_{\epsilon,{\rm nloc}}
operator is known to have issues at high wavenumbers
\sim 1/\epsilon
leading to nonsensical mapping properties
[
30
,
50
,
69
,
63
]
, even in analytic regularity. This issue does not typically affect numerics, as these high frequencies are truncated by discretization. Even so, from a PDE perspective,
\mathcal{L}^{\rm SB}_{\epsilon,{\rm nloc}}
cannot give rise to a well-posed evolution equation (
1
).

Thus, a central question is how to incorporate more of the fluid physics into the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
while still yielding a well-posed PDE evolution (
1
). In
[
52
,
53
]
, the author along with Mori and Spirn propose a novel candidate for the coupling operator
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
in the form of an angle-averaged Neumann-to-Dirichlet operator for the 3D Stokes equations. This notion of
slender body Neumann-to-Dirichlet (NtD) map
is defined as the solution to a natural type of boundary value problem for the Stokes equations in the exterior of the thin filament (see equation (
11
)). It therefore incorporates much more of the fluid physics than local slender body theory but also has more reasonable mapping properties than nonlocal slender body theory. In
[
52
,
53
]
, we establish well-posedness for the static slender body boundary value problem in a natural energy space and moreover show that the solution is close in a suitable sense to nonlocal slender body theory. We have since extended this notion of slender body NtD map in various directions
[
49
,
57
,
50
,
46
,
59
]
, but all analysis has thus far been in the static setting.

Here we give a more complete answer to the central question above by establishing local-in-time well-posedness for a simplified version of (
1
) (see equation (
20
)) using the slender body NtD map
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
. In our simplified dynamics, the filament is allowed to change length over time, although not necessarily in a physically realistic way. This allows us to develop the theory for the main part of the evolution (
1
) without also needing to solve for the filament tension
\tau
. Developing this well-posedness theory requires a detailed understanding of the mapping properties of the map
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
. The bulk of this paper is therefore devoted to proving Theorem
1.5
, a technically demanding decomposition of
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
into a main part plus lower order remainders which are smoother or small with respect to
\epsilon
.

Using a layer potential argument, we show that the principal behavior of
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
is that of the corresponding operator about a straight cylinder with periodic boundary conditions, for which we can derive an explicit symbol. Using this explicit symbol, we show that the toy extensible version of (
1
) is third-order parabolic and locally well-posed. This provides a proof-of-concept for using
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
to solve a dynamic free boundary problem, and represents an important step toward establishing well-posedness for the full evolution (
1
) and a more complete mathematical justification of slender body theories of filament dynamics in Stokes flow.

Our extraction of the straight operator as the leading order behavior of
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
is a type of small-scale decomposition analogous to similar Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator decompositions used in other interfacial fluids problems. These include Hele-Shaw and water waves problems
[
1
,
3
,
7
,
33
,
39
]
, the Muskat problem
[
2
,
22
,
56
]
, and the Peskin problem for the evolution of a 1D filament in 2D Stokes flow
[
12
,
14
,
25
,
27
,
42
,
38
,
54
,
66
,
67
,
26
]
. In many of these other interfacial fluids problems, however, the principal part of the operator is simpler to state and analyze. In contrast, the slender body NtD operator
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
requires a cylindrical geometry to be well-defined, and even for the straight cylinder, the symbol, while explicit, is not a simple expression.
Considering a curved, closed filament as a perturbation of the straight filament and showing that the local effects due to curvature are lower order has also been used as a strategy to study vortex filament solutions of the Ginzburg-Landau, Navier-Stokes, and Euler equations
[
8
,
19
,
20
]
. We will also frequently refer to the author’s earlier work
[
59
]
deriving an analogous operator decomposition in the much simpler setting of the Laplace equation.

1.1.
Geometry

We begin by defining the filament geometry at a fixed instant in time (for example, the initial configuration of the fiber). The geometric quantities described here will play a major role in the eventual extraction of the principal behavior for the slender body NtD map (Theorem
1.5
). At this fixed time, we let
\bm{X}:\,\mathbb{T}:=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
denote the arclength parameterization of a closed, unit-length curve
\Gamma_{0}
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
(see figure
1
). We require that the curve belongs to
C^{2,\beta}(\mathbb{T})
,
0<\beta<1
, and satisfies a non-self-intersection condition

(2)

\inf_{s\neq s^{\prime}}\frac{\left\lvert\bm{X}(s)-\bm{X}(s^{\prime})\right\rvert}{\left\lvert s-s^{\prime}\right\rvert}\geq c_{\Gamma}

for some constant
c_{\Gamma}>0
. Taking
\bm{e}_{\rm t}(s)=\frac{d\bm{X}}{ds}(s)
to be the unit tangent vector to the curve at
s
, points sufficiently close to
\Gamma_{0}
may be described by a
C^{1,\beta}
orthonormal frame
(\bm{e}_{\rm t}(s),\bm{e}_{\rm n_{1}}(s),\bm{e}_{\rm n_{2}}(s))
satisfying the ODEs

(3)

\frac{d}{ds}\begin{pmatrix}\bm{e}_{\rm t}(s)\\
\bm{e}_{\rm n_{1}}(s)\\
\bm{e}_{\rm n_{2}}(s)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\kappa_{1}(s)&\kappa_{2}(s)\\
-\kappa_{1}(s)&0&\kappa_{3}\\
-\kappa_{2}(s)&-\kappa_{3}&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\bm{e}_{\rm t}(s)\\
\bm{e}_{\rm n_{1}}(s)\\
\bm{e}_{\rm n_{2}}(s)\end{pmatrix}\,.

The coefficients
\kappa_{1}
and
\kappa_{2}
are related to the shape of the curve via

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}^{2}(s)+\kappa_{2}^{2}(s)=\kappa^{2}(s)\,,

where
\kappa^{2}(s)=\left\lvert\bm{X}_{ss}\right\rvert^{2}
is the squared curvature of
\bm{X}(s)
. The coefficient
\kappa_{3}
may be taken to be a constant satisfying
\left\lvert\kappa_{3}\right\rvert\leq\pi
(see
[
52
, Lemma 1.1]
). Throughout, we will use the notation

(4)

\kappa_{*}:=\sup_{s\in\mathbb{T}}\left\lvert\kappa(s)\right\rvert,\quad\kappa_{*,\beta}:=\sup_{s\neq s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}}\frac{\left\lvert\bm{X}_{ss}(s)-\bm{X}_{ss}(s^{\prime})\right\rvert}{\left\lvert s-s^{\prime}\right\rvert^{\beta}}+\kappa_{*}\,.

Note that, using (
3
), the coefficients
\kappa_{\ell}
,
\ell=1,2
, satisfy

\displaystyle\left\lVert\kappa_{\ell}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\beta}(\mathbb{T})}=\left\lVert\bm{X}_{ss}\cdot\bm{e}_{{\rm n}_{\ell}}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\beta}}\leq\kappa_{*,\beta}\,.

Given (
3
), it will also be convenient to define the following cylindrical basis vectors:

(5)

\bm{e}_{r}(s,\theta)=\cos\theta\bm{e}_{\rm n_{1}}(s)+\sin\theta\bm{e}_{\rm n_{2}}(s)\,,\quad\bm{e}_{\theta}(s,\theta)=-\sin\theta\bm{e}_{\rm n_{1}}(s)+\cos\theta\bm{e}_{\rm n_{2}}(s)\,.

Now, for
\Gamma_{0}
as above, there exists a maximal radius

(6)

0<r_{*}=r_{*}(c_{\Gamma},\kappa_{*})\leq\min\bigg\{\frac{1}{2\kappa_{*}},\frac{c_{\Gamma}}{2}\bigg\}

such that we may uniquely parameterize points
\bm{x}
satisfying
{\rm dist}(\bm{x},\Gamma_{0})<r_{*}
as

\displaystyle\bm{x}=\bm{X}(s)+r\bm{e}_{r}(s,\theta),\quad 0\leq r<r_{*}\,.

For any
0<\epsilon<\frac{r_{*}}{4}
, we may define a slender filament with uniform radius
\epsilon
as

(7)

\Sigma_{\epsilon}:=\big\{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\;:\;\bm{x}=\bm{X}(s)+r\bm{e}_{r}(s,\theta)\,,\;s\in\mathbb{T}\,,\;r<\epsilon\,,\;\theta\in 2\pi\mathbb{T}\big\}\,.

The filament surface
\Gamma_{\epsilon}=\partial\Sigma_{\epsilon}
is given by

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}:=\big\{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\;:\;\bm{x}=\bm{X}(s)+\epsilon\bm{e}_{r}(s,\theta)\,,\;s\in\mathbb{T}\,,\;\theta\in 2\pi\mathbb{T}\big\}\,,

with surface element
dS_{\epsilon}=\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}(s,\theta)\,d\theta ds
where
\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}(s,\theta)
is given by

(8)

\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}(s,\theta)=\epsilon(1-\epsilon\widehat{\kappa}(s,\theta))\,,\qquad\widehat{\kappa}(s,\theta):=\kappa_{1}(s)\cos\theta+\kappa_{2}(s)\sin\theta\,.

Figure 1
.
A typical curved filament
\Sigma_{\epsilon}
as in (
7
) in comparison with the straight filament
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
given by (
21
).

Let
\Sigma_{\epsilon}^{(a)}
and
\Sigma_{\epsilon}^{(b)}
be two closed filaments as in (
7
) with centerlines parameterized by
\bm{X}^{(a)}(s)
and
\bm{X}^{(b)}(s)
, respectively. We will be concerned with pairs of curves that are “close” in the following sense. Choose the basepoints
\bm{X}^{(a)}(0)
,
\bm{X}^{(b)}(0)
to be the nearest points on the two curves, i.e.

\displaystyle\min_{s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathbb{T}}\left\lvert\bm{X}^{(a)}(s_{1})-\bm{X}^{(b)}(s_{2})\right\rvert\,.

Note that there may be multiple pairs of nearest points, in which case we may then select one such pair arbitrarily. Given this choice of basepoint, we consider pairs of curves satisfying

(9)

\left\lVert\bm{X}^{(a)}-\bm{X}^{(b)}\right\rVert_{C^{2,\beta}(\mathbb{T})}=\delta

for
\delta\geq 0
sufficiently small.
For two such nearby curves, the corresponding orthonormal frames may also be chosen to be close. In particular, from (
9
) we have that
\bm{e}_{\rm t}^{(a)}=\frac{d\bm{X}^{(a)}}{ds}
and
\bm{e}_{\rm t}^{(b)}=\frac{d\bm{X}^{(b)}}{ds}
satisfy
\left\lVert\bm{e}_{\rm t}^{(a)}-\bm{e}_{\rm t}^{(b)}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\beta}(\mathbb{T})}\leq\delta
, and there exists a systematic choice of normal vectors along each curve such that the frames
(\bm{e}_{\rm t}^{(a)},\bm{e}_{\rm n_{1}}^{(a)},\bm{e}_{\rm n_{2}}^{(a)})
,
(\bm{e}_{\rm t}^{(b)},\bm{e}_{\rm n_{1}}^{(b)},\bm{e}_{\rm n_{2}}^{(b)})
both satisfy (
3
) and the following lemma holds.

Lemma 1.1

(Nearby parameterizations for nearby curves)
.

Given two closed filaments with centerlines
\bm{X}^{(a)}(s)
,
\bm{X}^{(b)}(s)
satisfying (
9
), there exists a choice of orthonormal frames
(\bm{e}_{\rm t}^{(a)},\bm{e}_{\rm n_{1}}^{(a)},\bm{e}_{\rm n_{2}}^{(a)})
and
(\bm{e}_{\rm t}^{(b)},\bm{e}_{\rm n_{1}}^{(b)},\bm{e}_{\rm n_{2}}^{(b)})
, respectively, satisfying (
3
) such that

(10)

\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}\left\lVert\kappa_{\ell}^{(a)}-\kappa_{\ell}^{(b)}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\beta}(\mathbb{T})}\leq c(\kappa_{*,\beta}^{(a)},\kappa_{*,\beta}^{(b)})\,\left\lVert\bm{X}^{(a)}-\bm{X}^{(b)}\right\rVert_{C^{2,\beta}(\mathbb{T})}\,.

The proof of Lemma
1.1
appears in Appendix
A
. Given two sufficiently nearby filaments, we will always choose a parameterization satisfying the estimate (
10
).

1.2.
Slender body Neumann-to-Dirichlet map

To study an evolution equation of the form (
1
), we must first define the map
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
being used to map force data defined along a 1D curve to a 3D velocity field governing the motion of the filament. Here we define
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
as the solution to the following
slender body boundary value problem
, first introduced by the author along with Mori and Spirn in
[
52
]
. Given force data
\bm{f}(s)
,
s\in\mathbb{T}
, we consider
(\bm{u},p)
satisfying

(11)

\displaystyle-\Delta\bm{u}+\nabla p

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad{\rm{div}\,}\bm{u}=0\qquad\text{in }\Omega_{\epsilon}=\mathbb{R}^{3}\backslash\overline{\Sigma_{\epsilon}}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}(\bm{\sigma}\bm{n})\,\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}(s,\theta)\,d\theta

\displaystyle=\bm{f}(s)\qquad\qquad\qquad\;\text{on }\Gamma_{\epsilon}

\displaystyle\bm{u}\big|_{\Gamma_{\epsilon}}

\displaystyle=\bm{v}(s)\,,\qquad\qquad\quad\;\;\,\text{unknown but independent of }\theta\,,

and
\left\lvert\bm{u}\right\rvert\to 0
as
\left\lvert\bm{x}\right\rvert\to\infty
. Here
\bm{\sigma}=\bm{\sigma}[\bm{u}]=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla\bm{u}+\nabla\bm{u}^{\rm T})-p{\bf I}
is the viscous stress tensor and
\bm{n}(\bm{x})
is the unit normal vector at
\bm{x}\in\Gamma_{\epsilon}
. The force data
\bm{f}(s)
is understood as the total surface stress
\bm{\sigma}[\bm{u}]\bm{n}
over each cross section of the filament, weighted by the fiber surface area via the Jacobian factor (
8
). The corresponding Dirichlet boundary value
\bm{v}(s)
along the filament surface is
a priori
unknown but constrained to be constant over each cross section of the fiber.

In
[
52
,
53
,
49
]
, the slender body BVP is shown to be well posed in a natural energy space (see section
6
for further details). Moreover, given
\bm{f}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{T})
, the solution
\bm{u}(\bm{x})
to (
11
) and its Dirichlet boundary value
\bm{v}(s)
are shown to be close in a quantitative sense to the corresponding nonlocal slender body theory expressions. In particular,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{v}-\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon,\rm nloc}^{\rm SB}[\bm{f}]\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq c(\kappa_{*},c_{\Gamma})\,\epsilon\left\lvert\log\epsilon\right\rvert^{3/2}\left\lVert\bm{f}\right\rVert_{C^{1}(\mathbb{T})}\,.

We define the Stokes
slender body Neumann-to-Dirichlet (NtD) map

\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
as the operator

(12)

\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}:\bm{f}(s)\mapsto\bm{v}(s)

mapping the total force per cross section
\bm{f}(s)
to the
\theta
-independent velocity
\bm{v}(s)
along
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
by solving the boundary value problem (
11
). Our analysis hinges on a detailed understanding of this map.
Given the nonlocal nature of the boundary conditions in (
11
), our analysis of
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
will rely heavily on the fact that the inverse map, the
slender body Dirichlet-to-Neumann (DtN) map
,

(13)

\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}^{-1}:\bm{v}(s)\mapsto\bm{f}(s)\,,

is much simpler to study. In particular,
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
is just the usual Stokes Dirichlet-to-Neumann map along a surface using
\theta
-independent Dirichlet data, with an additional step of angle-averaging the resulting Neumann data.

To study the maps
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
in detail, we will work in the setting of Hölder spaces along the filament surface
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
, as this will be a natural setting for analyzing the boundary integral formulation of (
11
) considered in section
1.2.4
. For a vector-valued function
\bm{h}
defined along
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
, we denote

(14)

\left\lVert\bm{h}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}=\esssup_{\bm{x}\in\Gamma_{\epsilon}}\left\lvert\bm{h}(\bm{x})\right\rvert\,,\qquad\left\lvert\bm{h}\right\rvert_{\dot{C}^{0,\alpha}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}=\sup_{\bm{x}\neq\bm{x}^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{\epsilon}}\frac{\left\lvert\bm{h}(\bm{x})-\bm{h}(\bm{x}^{\prime})\right\rvert}{\left\lvert\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}\right\rvert^{\alpha}}\,,\quad 0<\alpha<1\,,

and define the Hölder spaces
C^{k,\alpha}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})
as

(15)

C^{k,\alpha}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})=\{\bm{h}\;:\;\left\lVert\bm{h}\right\rVert_{C^{k,\alpha}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}<\infty\}\,,\qquad\left\lVert\bm{h}\right\rVert_{C^{k,\alpha}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\left\lVert\nabla^{\ell}\bm{h}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}+\left\lvert\nabla^{k}\bm{h}\right\rvert_{\dot{C}^{0,\alpha}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}\,.

1.2.1.
Evolution problem and theorem statement

Given the map
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
defined by solving the slender body boundary value problem (
11
), we consider a simplified version of (
1
) without the inextensibility constraint. This simplifies many aspects of the evolution, as we avoid the need to simultaneously solve a
tension determination problem
for
\tau(s,t)
along with the evolution equation for
\bm{X}

[
38
,
51
,
58
]
. This allows us to focus on the behavior of
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
as the main difficulty in this paper, and develop the theory for the main part of the evolution (
1
), namely,
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
applied to a fourth derivative of the filament centerline. The tension determination problem with
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
will be considered in future work.

However, we now have to consider a filament that is possibly growing or shrinking in length over time in a non-uniform way. To treat this possible filament length change in the simplest way, we choose to artificially introduce some rescalings such that the main operator always acts on a unit length curve. We emphasize that this choice of dynamics has the same main term as (
1
).

For an extensible filament, we will consider the evolution of
\bm{X}^{\sigma}(\sigma,t)
where
0\leq\sigma\leq 1
is now a material coordinate rather than an arclength coordinate. Let

(16)

\lambda(t)=\int_{0}^{1}\left\lvert\partial_{\sigma}\bm{X}^{\sigma}(\sigma,t)\right\rvert\,d\sigma

denote the length of the filament at time
t
. We will require the filament to have length 1 at the initial time, with
\left\lvert\partial_{\sigma}(\bm{X}^{\sigma})^{\rm in}\right\rvert=1
at each
\sigma
.

The NtD operator
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
depends on two arguments: a curve in arclength parameterization and a function of the arclength parameter. In (
11
), the problem is introduced for a unit length curve, but may be readily extended to more general curves. By making the change of variables
\widetilde{\bm{x}}=\lambda\bm{x}
in (
11
), we can see how
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
changes under uniform rescaling. For now, we suppress the time dependence in our notation. Let
\widetilde{s}=\lambda s
for
0\leq s\leq 1
and denote
\widetilde{\bm{v}}(\widetilde{s})=\bm{v}(s)
,
\widetilde{\bm{f}}(\widetilde{s})=\bm{f}(s)
, and
\widetilde{\bm{X}}(\widetilde{s})=\bm{X}(s)
. We then have

(17)

\mathcal{L}_{\lambda\epsilon}(\widetilde{\bm{X}})[\widetilde{\bm{f}}](\widetilde{s})=\lambda^{-1}\widetilde{\bm{v}}(\widetilde{s})=\lambda^{-1}\bm{v}(s)=\lambda^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}(\bm{X})[\bm{f}](s)\,.

We will consider the evolution

(18)

\frac{\partial\bm{X}^{\sigma}}{\partial t}(\sigma,t)=\mathcal{L}_{\lambda\epsilon}(\widetilde{\bm{X}})[\widetilde{\bm{f}}(\widetilde{s},t)](\lambda\sigma)\,,

where
\bm{X}^{\sigma}
parameterizes the curve of interest and
\widetilde{\bm{X}}
is the same curve parameterized by arclength
\widetilde{s}
. Here we will use the forcing term

(19)

\widetilde{\bm{f}}(\widetilde{s},t)=-(\partial_{\sigma}^{4}\bm{X}^{\sigma})\big|_{\sigma=\widetilde{s}/\lambda}\,,

i.e., we take the fourth derivative with respect to the material parameter and evaluate at the rescaled arclength parameter
0\leq\widetilde{s}/\lambda(t)\leq 1
, where the rescaling occurs around a fixed basepoint
\bm{X}(0,t)
. Note that the material points and rescaled arclength parameter do not necessarily match up precisely after the initial time. An additional nonphysical feature is that for convenience we will take a fixed
ratio

\epsilon
between the filament radius and length, rather than having a fixed filament radius.

Putting (
17
), (
18
), and (
19
) together, we obtain the curve evolution

(20)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\bm{X}^{\sigma}}{\partial t}(\sigma,t)

\displaystyle=-\lambda(t)^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}(\widetilde{R}_{1}[\bm{X}^{\sigma}])\big[\partial_{\sigma}^{4}\bm{X}^{\sigma}\big]\,,

\displaystyle\bm{X}^{\sigma}(\sigma,0)

\displaystyle=(\bm{X}^{\sigma})^{\rm in}(\sigma)\,,\qquad\left\lvert\partial_{\sigma}(\bm{X}^{\sigma})^{\rm in}\right\rvert=1\,,

\displaystyle\lambda(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\left\lvert\partial_{\sigma}\bm{X}^{\sigma}\right\rvert\,d\sigma\,.

Here we use
\widetilde{R}_{1}[\bm{X}^{\sigma}](s)
to denote the arclength parameterization of the uniform rescaling of the curve
\bm{X}^{\sigma}
to length 1.
We show the following.

Theorem 1.2

(Local well-posedness for filament evolution)
.

Given
(\bm{X}^{\sigma})^{\rm in}\in C^{4,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})
satisfying (
2
), there exists
\epsilon_{0}>0
depending only on
\left\lVert(\bm{X}^{\sigma})^{\rm in}\right\rVert_{C^{4,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}
and
c_{\Gamma}
such that for
0<\epsilon<\epsilon_{0}
, there exists a time
T>0
depending on
\epsilon
,
\left\lVert(\bm{X}^{\sigma})^{\rm in}\right\rVert_{C^{4,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}
, and
c_{\Gamma}
such that the evolution (
20
) admits a unique solution
\bm{X}^{\sigma}(\sigma,t)\in L^{\infty}(0,T;C^{4,\alpha}(\mathbb{T}))
.

The proof of Theorem
1.2
in section
2.2
is relatively straightforward by design. It serves mainly as a proof-of-concept for the use of the slender body NtD operator
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
for a dynamic curve evolution problem. The main content of this paper is a detailed decomposition of
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
that we will introduce in the next sections and summarize in Theorem
1.5
. The decomposition of
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}=\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}(\bm{X})
will be presented for a unit length centerline curve
\bm{X}(s)
in arclength parameterization. We will typically suppress the curve dependence of the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
in our notation. The material parameter
\sigma
and curve
\bm{X}^{\sigma}
will only be used to define the evolution (
20
) and prove Theorem
1.2
; everything else will be in terms of
\bm{X}(s)
.

1.2.2.
Slender body NtD map for the straight cylinder

The proof of Theorem
1.2
relies heavily on the fact that we can derive very detailed information about the slender body NtD and its inverse, the slender body DtN, for the simple geometry of a straight cylinder with periodic boundary conditions. We define

(21)

\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}:=\big\{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}\;:\;\bm{x}=s\bm{e}_{z}+\epsilon\bm{e}_{r}(\theta)\,,\;s\in\mathbb{T}\,,\;\theta\in 2\pi\mathbb{T}\big\}\,.

Let
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}
and
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
denote the slender body NtD and DtN maps along
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
. Due to symmetry, the behavior of
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}
and
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
in this simple geometry decouples into directions tangential (
\bm{e}_{z}
) and normal (
\bm{e}_{x},\bm{e}_{y}
) to the filament centerline. The eigenfunctions of
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}
and
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
in each direction are then just
e^{2\pi iks}\bm{e}_{z}
,
e^{2\pi iks}\bm{e}_{x}
,
e^{2\pi iks}\bm{e}_{y}
, and we may consider the eigenvalues
m_{\epsilon,{\rm t}}^{-1}(k)
and
m_{\epsilon,{\rm n}}^{-1}(k)
of the slender body DtN
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
satisfying

(22)

\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}[e^{2\pi iks}\bm{e}_{z}]=m_{\epsilon,{\rm t}}^{-1}(k)e^{2\pi iks}\bm{e}_{z}\,,\qquad\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}[e^{2\pi iks}\bm{e}_{x}]=m_{\epsilon,{\rm n}}^{-1}(k)e^{2\pi iks}\bm{e}_{x}\,.

Note that by symmetry the
\bm{e}_{y}
direction is identical to the
\bm{e}_{x}
direction.
In
[
50
]
, the following explicit expressions are calculated for
m_{\epsilon,{\rm t}}^{-1}(k)
and
m_{\epsilon,{\rm n}}^{-1}(k)
.

Proposition 1.3

(Slender body DtN spectrum)
.

The eigenvalues
m_{\epsilon,{\rm t}}^{-1}(k)
,
m_{\epsilon,{\rm n}}^{-1}(k)
of the straight slender body DtN map
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
satisfying (
22
) are given by

(23)

\displaystyle m_{\epsilon,{\rm t}}^{-1}(k)

\displaystyle=\frac{8\pi^{2}\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert K_{1}^{2}}{2K_{0}K_{1}+2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert\big(K_{0}^{2}-K_{1}^{2}\big)}

(24)

\displaystyle m_{\epsilon,{\rm n}}^{-1}(k)

\displaystyle=4\pi^{2}\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert\frac{4K_{1}^{2}K_{2}+2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert K_{1}(K_{1}^{2}-K_{0}K_{2})}{2K_{0}K_{1}K_{2}+2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert\big(K_{1}^{2}(K_{0}+K_{2})-2K_{0}^{2}K_{2}\big)}

where each
K_{j}=K_{j}(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)
,
j=0,1,2
, is a
j^{\text{t}h}
order modified Bessel function of the second kind.

In
[
50
]
, both
m_{\epsilon,{\rm t}}^{-1}(k)
and
m_{\epsilon,{\rm n}}^{-1}(k)
are shown to grow like
\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert
as
\left\lvert k\right\rvert\to\infty
; in particular,
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}(\mathbb{T})
maps the Sobolev space
H^{m}
to
H^{m-1}(\mathbb{T})
. In addition, at low wavenumbers
\left\lvert k\right\rvert\lesssim\frac{1}{\epsilon}
, both
m_{\epsilon,{\rm t}}^{-1}(k)
and
m_{\epsilon,{\rm n}}^{-1}(k)
scale as
\left\lvert\log(\epsilon|k|)\right\rvert^{-1}
(see figure
2
). Here we will be working in Hölder spaces and thus will require a more detailed characterization of the behavior of
m_{\epsilon,{\rm t}}^{-1}(k)
and
m_{\epsilon,{\rm n}}^{-1}(k)
. We use the explicit expressions (
23
) and (
24
) to show the following.

Lemma 1.4

(Mapping properties of SB NtD and DtN along
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
)
.

Let
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}
denote the slender body NtD map (
12
) along the straight filament
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
. Given total force data
\bm{f}(s)\in C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})
along
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
with
\int_{\mathbb{T}}\bm{f}(s)\,ds=0
, we have

(25)

\left\lVert\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}[\bm{f}]\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}\leq c\left\lvert\log\epsilon\right\rvert\big(\left\lVert\bm{f}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}+\epsilon^{-1}\left\lvert\bm{f}\right\rvert_{\dot{C}^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}\big)\,.

Letting
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
denote the SB DtN map (
13
) along
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
, given a
\theta
-independent velocity field
\bm{v}(s)\in C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})
along
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
, we have

(26)

\left\lVert\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}[\bm{v}]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}\leq c\left\lvert\log\epsilon\right\rvert^{-1}\left\lVert\bm{v}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}\,.

The proof of Lemma
1.4
appears in section
3.4
.

1.2.3.
Decomposition of the slender body DtN and NtD maps

The proof of Theorem
1.2
relies on a detailed decomposition of the slender body DtN and NtD maps for arbitrarily curved filaments. In particular, given a curved filament, we extract the straight operators
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
,
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}
as the leading order behavior and show that the effects of curvature are lower order: either small in
\epsilon
or more regular. Given the explicit expressions for the behavior of
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
and
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}
, this decomposition will allow us to go on to prove local well-posedness for the evolution (
20
).

In order to accomplish this, we will need to define a way of identifying vector valued functions defined on curvilinear basis vectors
(\bm{e}_{\rm t}(s),\bm{e}_{\rm n_{1}}(s),\bm{e}_{\rm n_{2}}(s))
along
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
with vector valued functions defined on
(\bm{e}_{z},\bm{e}_{x},\bm{e}_{y})
along
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
. We define a map
\Phi
identifying tangential and normal components of vectors along
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
with tangential and normal components of vectors along
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
. In particular, given a vector-valued function
\bm{g}
along the straight filament
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
and vector-valued
\bm{h}
about the curved filament
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
, we identify the tangential and normal components in the straight versus curved setting via

(27)

\displaystyle\Phi\bm{g}

\displaystyle=(\bm{g}\cdot\bm{e}_{z})\bm{e}_{\rm t}+(\bm{g}\cdot\bm{e}_{x})\bm{e}_{\rm n_{1}}+(\bm{g}\cdot\bm{e}_{y})\bm{e}_{\rm n_{2}}

\displaystyle\Phi^{-1}\bm{h}

\displaystyle=(\bm{h}\cdot\bm{e}_{\rm t})\bm{e}_{z}+(\bm{h}\cdot\bm{e}_{\rm n_{1}})\bm{e}_{x}+(\bm{h}\cdot\bm{e}_{\rm n_{2}})\bm{e}_{y}\,.

For any
\bm{h}
defined along
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
, we define

(28)

\bm{h}_{0}^{\Phi}:=\bm{h}-\Phi\int_{\mathbb{T}}(\Phi^{-1}\bm{h})\,ds\,;

in particular,
\Phi^{-1}\bm{h}_{0}^{\Phi}
is mean-zero in
s
along the straight filament
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
.

Given the definition (
27
) of
\Phi
, we show the following.

Theorem 1.5

(Decomposition of SB DtN and NtD)
.

Let
0<\alpha<\gamma<\beta<1
and consider a closed filament
\Sigma_{\epsilon}
with centerline
\bm{X}(s)\in C^{2,\beta}(\mathbb{T})
. Let
\bm{v}(s)\in C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})
be
\theta
-independent Dirichlet data along
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
. There exists
\epsilon_{\rm d}=\epsilon_{\rm d}(c_{\Gamma},\kappa_{*})>0
such that for
0<\epsilon\leq\epsilon_{\rm d}
, the slender body Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
as defined in (
13
) may be decomposed as

(29)

\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}^{-1}[\bm{v}](s)=\Phi\overline{\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}}^{-1}[\Phi^{-1}\bm{v}(s)]+\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,\epsilon}[\bm{v}(s)]+\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,+}[\bm{v}(s)]

where the remainder terms satisfy

(30)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,\epsilon}[\bm{v}]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq c(\kappa_{*,\alpha^{+}},c_{\Gamma})\,\epsilon^{2-\alpha^{+}}\left\lVert\bm{v}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,+}[\bm{v}]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq c(\epsilon,\kappa_{*,\gamma^{+}},c_{\Gamma})\left\lVert\bm{v}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}

for any
\alpha^{+}\in(\alpha,\beta]
and
\gamma^{+}\in(\gamma,\beta]
.
Furthermore, given two nearby filaments with centerlines
\bm{X}^{(a)}(s)
,
\bm{X}^{(b)}(s)
satisfying Lemma
1.1
, the differences between their corresponding remainder terms
\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,\epsilon}^{(a)}-\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,\epsilon}^{(b)}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,+}^{(a)}-\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,+}^{(b)}
satisfy

(31)

\displaystyle\left\lVert(\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,\epsilon}^{(a)}-\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,\epsilon}^{(b)})[\bm{v}]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq c(\kappa_{*,\alpha^{+}}^{(a)},\kappa_{*,\alpha^{+}}^{(b)},c_{\Gamma})\,\epsilon^{2-\alpha^{+}}\left\lVert\bm{X}^{(a)}-\bm{X}^{(b)}\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha^{+}}(\mathbb{T})}\left\lVert\bm{v}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\left\lVert(\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,+}^{(a)}-\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,+}^{(b)})[\bm{v}]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq c(\epsilon,\kappa_{*,\gamma^{+}}^{(a)},\kappa_{*,\gamma^{+}}^{(b)},c_{\Gamma})\left\lVert\bm{X}^{(a)}-\bm{X}^{(b)}\right\rVert_{C^{2,\gamma^{+}}(\mathbb{T})}\left\lVert\bm{v}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}\,.

For a filament
\Sigma_{\epsilon}
with centerline
\bm{X}(s)\in C^{3,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})
, let
\bm{f}(s)\in C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})
be slender body force data. There exists
\epsilon_{\rm n}=\epsilon_{\rm n}(c_{\Gamma},\kappa_{*,\gamma})>0
such that for
0<\epsilon\leq\epsilon_{\rm n}
, the slender body Neumann-to-Dirichlet operator
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
given by (
12
) may be similarly decomposed as

(32)

\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}[\bm{f}](s)=\big({\bf I}+\mathcal{R}_{\rm n,\epsilon}\big)\big[\Phi\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}[\Phi^{-1}\bm{f}_{0}^{\Phi}(s)]\big]+\mathcal{R}_{\rm n,+}[\bm{f}(s)]

where the remainder terms satisfy

(33)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{\rm n,\epsilon}[\bm{g}]\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq c(\kappa_{*,\alpha^{+}},c_{\Gamma})\,\epsilon^{1-\alpha^{+}}\left\lvert\log\epsilon\right\rvert\left\lVert\bm{g}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{\rm n,+}[\bm{f}]\right\rVert_{C^{1,\gamma}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq c(\epsilon,\|\bm{X}_{ss}\|_{C^{1,\alpha}},c_{\Gamma})\left\lVert\bm{f}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}

for any
\alpha^{+}\in(\alpha,\beta]
.
Given two nearby filaments with centerlines
\bm{X}^{(a)}(s)
,
\bm{X}^{(b)}(s)
in
C^{3,\alpha}
and satisfying Lemma
1.1
, we also have

(34)

\displaystyle\left\lVert(\mathcal{R}_{\rm n,\epsilon}^{(a)}-\mathcal{R}_{\rm n,\epsilon}^{(b)})[\bm{g}]\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq c(\kappa_{*,\alpha^{+}}^{(a)},\kappa_{*,\alpha^{+}}^{(b)},c_{\Gamma})\,\epsilon^{1-\alpha^{+}}\left\lvert\log\epsilon\right\rvert\left\lVert\bm{X}^{(a)}-\bm{X}^{(b)}\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha^{+}}}\left\lVert\bm{g}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\left\lVert(\mathcal{R}_{\rm n,+}^{(a)}-\mathcal{R}_{\rm n,+}^{(b)})[\bm{f}]\right\rVert_{C^{1,\gamma}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq c(\epsilon,\|\bm{X}_{ss}^{(a)}\|_{C^{1,\alpha}},\|\bm{X}_{ss}^{(b)}\|_{C^{1,\alpha}},c_{\Gamma})\left\lVert\bm{X}^{(a)}-\bm{X}^{(b)}\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha^{+}}}\left\lVert\bm{f}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}\,.

For both
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
, the
\epsilon
-dependence is explicit in the bound for the remainder terms
\mathcal{R}_{j,\epsilon}
but not for the smoother remainders
\mathcal{R}_{j,+}
.
Although only the decomposition (
32
) of
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
is required to show Theorem
1.2
, the proof of this representation is dependent on the decomposition (
29
) of
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
, which is simpler to study and forms the bulk of this paper. We thus state both results together.

Given force data
\bm{f}(s)
involving multiple derivatives in
s
, as in (
1
), we may refine the decomposition (
32
) of
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
as follows.

Corollary 1.6

(SB NtD decomposition involving derivatives)
.

For
0<\alpha<\gamma<1
, given
\bm{X}(s)\in C^{3,\gamma}(\mathbb{T})
and slender body force data of the form
\bm{f}(s)=\partial_{s}^{4}\bm{F}
for some
\bm{F}\in C^{4,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})
, the decomposition (
32
) of
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
simplifies to

(35)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}[\partial_{s}^{4}\bm{F}]

\displaystyle=({\bf I}+\mathcal{R}_{\rm n,\epsilon})\big[\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}[\partial_{s}^{4}\bm{F}]\big]+\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{\rm n,+}[\partial_{s}^{4}\bm{F}]\,,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{\rm n,\epsilon}\big[\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}[\partial_{s}^{4}\bm{F}]\big]\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq c(\|\bm{X}\|_{C^{2,\alpha^{+}}},c_{\Gamma})\,\epsilon^{1-\alpha^{+}}\left\lvert\log\epsilon\right\rvert\left\lVert\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}[\partial_{s}^{4}\bm{F}]\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\left\lVert\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{\rm n,+}[\partial_{s}^{4}\bm{F}]\right\rVert_{C^{1,\gamma}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq c(\epsilon,\|\bm{X}\|_{C^{3,\gamma}},c_{\Gamma})\left\lVert\bm{F}\right\rVert_{C^{4,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}\,,

where
\alpha<\alpha^{+}<1
.
Furthermore, given two nearby filaments with centerlines
\bm{X}^{(a)}(s)
,
\bm{X}^{(b)}(s)
in
C^{3,\gamma}
satisfying Lemma
1.1
, we have

(36)

\displaystyle\left\lVert(\mathcal{R}_{\rm n,\epsilon}^{(a)}-\mathcal{R}_{\rm n,\epsilon}^{(b)})[\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}[\partial_{s}^{4}\bm{F}]]\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq c(\|\bm{X}^{(a)}\|_{C^{2,\alpha^{+}}},\|\bm{X}^{(b)}\|_{C^{2,\alpha^{+}}},c_{\Gamma})\,\epsilon^{1-\alpha^{+}}\left\lvert\log\epsilon\right\rvert\left\lVert\bm{X}^{(a)}-\bm{X}^{(b)}\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha^{+}}(\mathbb{T})}\left\lVert\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}[\partial_{s}^{4}\bm{F}]\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\left\lVert(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{\rm n,+}^{(a)}-\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{\rm n,+}^{(b)})[\partial_{s}^{4}\bm{F}]\right\rVert_{C^{1,\gamma}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq c(\epsilon,\|\bm{X}^{(a)}\|_{C^{3,\gamma}},\|\bm{X}^{(b)}\|_{C^{3,\gamma}},c_{\Gamma})\left\lVert\bm{X}^{(a)}-\bm{X}^{(b)}\right\rVert_{C^{3,\gamma}(\mathbb{T})}\left\lVert\bm{F}\right\rVert_{C^{4,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}\,.

The proof of Theorem
1.5
relies on a series of key lemmas, which we will state first before proving Theorem
1.5
in section
2.1
. The proof of Corollary
1.6
will appear immediately after the proof of Theorem
1.5
. The proofs of each of these key lemmas then form the remainder of the paper.

1.2.4.
Boundary integral formulation of slender body DtN map

Motivated by our analysis in the Laplace setting
[
59
]
, the strategy for understanding the slender body NtD map will rely on an explicit boundary integral representation for the inverse map, the slender body DtN map, for which we can more easily obtain estimates.

We use
\mathcal{G}(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})
to denote the Stokeslet, the free-space Green’s function for the Stokes equations, given by the matrix

(37)

\mathcal{G}(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})=\frac{1}{8\pi}\bigg(\frac{{\bf I}}{\left\lvert\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}\right\rvert}+\frac{(\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime})\otimes(\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime})}{\left\lvert\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}\right\rvert^{3}}\bigg).

For
\bm{x}\in\Gamma_{\epsilon}
, let
\bm{n}(\bm{x})
denote the unit normal vector to
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
pointing
out from
the slender body
\Sigma_{\epsilon}
,
into
the fluid domain
\Omega_{\epsilon}
. We additionally define the stresslet kernel
1
1

1

Some sources
[
62
]
instead use the convention that the double layer kernel is given by
-\bm{K}_{\mathcal{D}}(\bm{x}^{\prime},\bm{x};\bm{n}(\bm{x}^{\prime}))
.

(38)

\bm{K}_{\mathcal{D}}(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime};\bm{n}(\bm{x}^{\prime}))=\frac{3}{4\pi}\frac{(\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime})\otimes(\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime})}{\left\lvert\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}\right\rvert^{5}}(\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime})\cdot\bm{n}(\bm{x}^{\prime})\,.

Given a continuous
\bm{\varphi}:\Gamma_{\epsilon}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
, for
\bm{x}\in\Omega_{\epsilon}
, we define the single layer operator
\mathcal{S}
and double layer operator
\mathcal{D}
as

(39)

\mathcal{S}[\bm{\varphi}](\bm{x})=\int_{\Gamma_{\epsilon}}\mathcal{G}(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})\bm{\varphi}(\bm{x}^{\prime})\,dS_{x^{\prime}}\,,\qquad\mathcal{D}[\bm{\varphi}](\bm{x})=\int_{\Gamma_{\epsilon}}\bm{K}_{\mathcal{D}}(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime};\bm{n}(\bm{x}^{\prime}))\bm{\varphi}(\bm{x}^{\prime})\,dS_{x^{\prime}}\,.

As in the Laplace setting
[
37
,
65
,
23
]
, the single layer operator is continuous up to
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
, while the double layer operator satisfies the exterior jump relation (see
[
62
, eq. (2.3.12)]
):

(40)

\lim_{h\to 0}\mathcal{D}[\bm{\varphi}](\bm{x}+h\bm{n}(\bm{x}))=\mathcal{D}[\bm{\varphi}](\bm{x})+\frac{1}{2}\bm{\varphi}(\bm{x})\,.

Following
[
62
, eq. (2.3.10)]
, the Stokes flow
\bm{u}(\bm{x})
at a point
\bm{x}\in\Omega_{\epsilon}
may be represented by the boundary integral expression

(41)

\bm{u}(\bm{x})=\mathcal{S}[\bm{w}](\bm{x})+\mathcal{D}[\bm{v}](\bm{x})

where, for
\bm{x}\in\Gamma_{\epsilon}
,

(42)

\displaystyle\bm{w}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=-\bm{\sigma}[\bm{u}]\bm{n}(\bm{x})\big|_{\Gamma_{\epsilon}}\,,

\displaystyle\bm{v}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\bm{u}\big|_{\Gamma_{\epsilon}}\,.

The negative sign in
\bm{w}
arises due to the convention that the normal vector
\bm{n}(\bm{x})
points into the fluid domain
\Omega_{\epsilon}
, making
-\bm{\sigma}[\bm{u}]\bm{n}(\bm{x})
the Neumann boundary value for
\bm{u}
. Using (
40
) in (
41
), on the filament surface
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
, we obtain a relation between the Dirichlet (
\bm{v}
) and Neumann (
\bm{w}
) boundary values for
\bm{u}
:

(43)

\textstyle\frac{1}{2}\bm{v}(\bm{x})-\mathcal{D}[\bm{v}](\bm{x})=\mathcal{S}[\bm{w}](\bm{x})\,,\qquad\bm{x}\in\Gamma_{\epsilon}\,.

Using (
43
), we may thus relate the slender body Dirichlet data
\bm{v}(s)
and angle-averaged Neumann data
\bm{f}(s)
from (
11
) as

(44)

\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{1}{2}\bm{v}(s)-\mathcal{D}[\bm{v}(s)]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{S}[\bm{w}(s,\theta)]

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\bm{w}(s,\theta)\,\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}(s,\theta)\,d\theta

\displaystyle=\bm{f}(s)

As in the Laplace setting
[
59
]
, we consider
\bm{w}(s,\theta)=\bm{w}(\bm{x}(s,\theta))
as a function of the parameters
s
and
\theta
along the surface
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
.

Accordingly, to better compare across different filament centerline geometries, we use the following characterization of the
\left\lvert\cdot\right\rvert_{\dot{C}^{0,\alpha}}
seminorm along
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
:

(45)

\left\lvert\bm{h}\right\rvert_{\dot{C}^{0,\alpha}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}=\sup_{(s-s^{\prime})^{2}+(\theta-\theta^{\prime})^{2}\neq 0}\frac{\left\lvert\bm{h}(s,\theta)-\bm{h}(s^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})\right\rvert}{\sqrt{(s-s^{\prime})^{2}+\epsilon^{2}(\theta-\theta^{\prime})^{2}}^{\,\alpha}}\,.

The seminorm (
45
) may be seen to be an equivalent characterization of
\dot{C}^{0,\alpha}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})
to (
15
)
(see
[
59
, Lemma 3.1]
). In addition, it will be useful to define the following spaces measuring additional Hölder regularity in
s
only:

(46)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{h}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}_{\rm s}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}

\displaystyle:=\left\lVert\bm{h}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}+\sup_{\theta\in[0,2\pi)}\bigg(\sup_{s\neq s^{\prime}}\frac{\left\lvert\bm{h}(s,\theta)-\bm{h}(s^{\prime},\theta)\right\rvert}{\left\lvert s-s^{\prime}\right\rvert^{\alpha}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{h}\right\rVert_{C^{m,\alpha}_{\rm s}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\ell=0}^{m}\left\lVert\partial_{s}^{\ell}\bm{h}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}+\sup_{\theta\in[0,2\pi)}\bigg(\sup_{s\neq s^{\prime}}\frac{\left\lvert\partial_{s}^{m}\bm{h}(s,\theta)-\partial_{s}^{m}\bm{h}(s^{\prime},\theta)\right\rvert}{\left\lvert s-s^{\prime}\right\rvert^{\alpha}}\bigg)\,.

Note that if
\bm{h}\in C^{m,\alpha}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})
is a function of
s
only, then both (
45
) and (
46
) reduce to

\displaystyle\left\lvert\bm{h}(s)\right\rvert_{\dot{C}^{0,\alpha}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}=\left\lvert\bm{h}(s)\right\rvert_{\dot{C}^{0,\alpha}_{s}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}=\left\lvert\bm{h}(s)\right\rvert_{\dot{C}^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}=\sup_{s\neq s^{\prime}}\frac{\left\lvert\bm{h}(s)-\bm{h}(s^{\prime})\right\rvert}{\left\lvert s-s^{\prime}\right\rvert^{\,\alpha}}\,.

1.2.5.
Single and double layer operators about
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}

In the Laplace setting
[
59
]
, we were able to calculate explicit Fourier multiplier expressions for the corresponding single and double layer potentials about the straight cylinder
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
. These expressions were then applied as maps between
\theta
-independent scalar-valued functions on
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
.

In the Stokes setting, we again may calculate explicit Fourier multiplier expressions for the single and double layer operators about the straight filament
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
, which we will denote by
\overline{\mathcal{S}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
, respectively. However, the situation is far more complicated. In particular, the multipliers are now matrix-valued expressions that must be applied between vector-valued functions living on cylindrical basis vectors
\bm{e}_{r}
,
\bm{e}_{\theta}
,
\bm{e}_{z}
.

Furthermore, the
\theta
-independent functions on
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
are represented not just by zero Fourier modes in
\theta
, but also by one modes: given a
\theta
-independent vector-valued function
\bm{f}(s)
along the straight cylinder
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
, for
s\in\mathbb{T}
we may write

\displaystyle\bm{f}(s)

\displaystyle=f^{z}(s)\,\bm{e}_{z}+f^{x}(s)\,\bm{e}_{x}+f^{y}(s)\,\bm{e}_{y}

\displaystyle=f^{z}(s)\,\bm{e}_{z}+f^{x}(s)(\cos\theta\bm{e}_{r}-\sin\theta\bm{e}_{\theta})+f^{y}(s)(\sin\theta\bm{e}_{r}+\cos\theta\bm{e}_{\theta})\,.

Along
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
, we may treat the tangential (
\bm{e}_{z}
) and normal (
\bm{e}_{x}
and
\bm{e}_{y}
) directions along the filament separately. Expanding each of
f^{z}(s)
,
f^{x}(s)
, and
f^{y}(s)
as a Fourier series in
s
, we show that the double and single layer operators
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{S}}
act in the following way.

Lemma 1.7

(Single and double layer multipliers about
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
)
.

When applied to
\theta
-independent data in the tangential direction along the straight cylinder
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
, the double layer operator
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
is given by

(47)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{D}}[e^{2\pi iks}\bm{e}_{z}]

\displaystyle=Q_{\rm tE}(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)\,e^{2\pi iks}\bm{e}_{z}+i\,Q_{\rm tF}(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)\,e^{2\pi iks}\bm{e}_{r}\,,

\displaystyle Q_{\rm tE}(z)

\displaystyle=\textstyle z^{2}\big(I_{0}K_{0}-I_{1}K_{1}\big)-\frac{z}{2}\big(I_{0}K_{1}-I_{1}K_{0}\big)\,,

\displaystyle Q_{\rm tF}(z)

\displaystyle=-z^{2}\big(I_{1}K_{0}-I_{0}K_{1}\big)-zI_{1}K_{1}\,.

Here and throughout, each
I_{j}=I_{j}(z)
and
K_{j}=K_{j}(z)
are modified Bessel functions of the first and second kind, respectively.
Given the form (
47
) of the double layer operator, let

(48)

\bm{F}_{z}=\big(f_{z}^{z}\bm{e}_{z}+f_{r}^{z}\bm{e}_{r}\big)e^{2\pi iks}\,.

When applied to data of the form (
48
), the single layer operator
\overline{\mathcal{S}}
is invertible and satisfies

(49)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}[\bm{F}_{z}]

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{-1}\bm{M}_{\rm S,t}^{-1}(k)\bm{F}_{z}\,,

\displaystyle\bm{M}_{\rm S,t}^{-1}(k)

\displaystyle=m_{\epsilon,{\rm tA}}(k)\,\bm{e}_{z}\otimes\bm{e}_{z}+i\,m_{\epsilon,{\rm tB}}(k)(\bm{e}_{r}\otimes\bm{e}_{z}-\bm{e}_{z}\otimes\bm{e}_{r})+m_{\epsilon,{\rm tC}}(k)\,\bm{e}_{r}\otimes\bm{e}_{r}\,.

The components of the matrix-valued multiplier
\bm{M}_{\rm S,t}^{-1}(k)
are given by

(50)

\displaystyle m_{\epsilon,{\rm t}j}(k)

\displaystyle=\frac{Q_{{\rm t}j}(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)}{Q_{\rm tD}(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)}\,,\quad j={\rm A,B,C}

\displaystyle Q_{\rm tA}(z)

\displaystyle=\textstyle\frac{1}{I_{0}K_{0}}\left(1+\frac{z}{2}\left(\frac{K_{0}}{K_{1}}-\frac{I_{0}}{I_{1}}\right)\right)\,,\quad Q_{\rm tC}(z)=\frac{1}{I_{1}K_{1}}\left(1+\frac{z}{2}\left(\frac{I_{1}}{I_{0}}-\frac{K_{1}}{K_{0}}\right)\right)\,,

\displaystyle Q_{\rm tB}(z)

\displaystyle=\textstyle\frac{z}{2}\left(\frac{1}{I_{1}K_{1}}-\frac{1}{I_{0}K_{0}}\right)\,,\quad Q_{\rm tD}(z)=\left(1+\frac{z}{2}\left(\frac{I_{1}}{I_{0}}-\frac{I_{0}}{I_{1}}\right)\right)\left(1+\frac{z}{2}\left(\frac{K_{0}}{K_{1}}-\frac{K_{1}}{K_{0}}\right)\right)\,.

When applied to
\theta
-independent data in the normal direction to the straight cylinder
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
, the double layer operator
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
is given by

(51)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{D}}[e^{2\pi iks}\bm{e}_{x}]

\displaystyle=\overline{\mathcal{D}}[e^{2\pi iks}\big(\cos\theta\bm{e}_{r}-\sin\theta\bm{e}_{\theta}\big)]

\displaystyle=\left(Q_{\rm nN}(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)\cos\theta\bm{e}_{r}-Q_{\rm nO}(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)\sin\theta\bm{e}_{\theta}+iQ_{\rm nP}(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)\cos\theta\bm{e}_{z}\right)e^{2\pi iks}\,,

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{D}}[e^{2\pi iks}\bm{e}_{y}]

\displaystyle=\overline{\mathcal{D}}[e^{2\pi iks}\big(\sin\theta\bm{e}_{r}+\cos\theta\bm{e}_{\theta}\big)]

\displaystyle=\left(Q_{\rm nN}(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)\sin\theta\bm{e}_{r}+Q_{\rm nO}(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)\cos\theta\bm{e}_{\theta}+iQ_{\rm nP}(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)\sin\theta\bm{e}_{z}\right)e^{2\pi iks}\,;

\displaystyle Q_{\rm nN}(z)

\displaystyle=z^{2}(I_{1}K_{1}-I_{0}K_{0})+\textstyle\frac{3}{2}z(I_{1}K_{0}-I_{0}K_{1})+2I_{1}K_{1}\,,

\displaystyle Q_{\rm nO}(z)

\displaystyle=\textstyle-\frac{z}{2}(I_{1}K_{0}-I_{0}K_{1})-2I_{1}K_{1}\,,\qquad Q_{\rm nP}(z)=z^{2}(I_{0}K_{1}-I_{1}K_{0})-zI_{1}K_{1}\,.

Given the form of the expression (
51
), let

(52)

\displaystyle\bm{F}_{x}(s,\theta)

\displaystyle=\big(f_{r}^{x}\cos\theta\bm{e}_{r}+f_{\theta}^{x}\sin\theta\bm{e}_{\theta}+f_{z}^{x}\cos\theta\bm{e}_{z}\big)e^{2\pi iks}

\displaystyle\bm{F}_{y}(s,\theta)

\displaystyle=\big(f_{r}^{y}\sin\theta\bm{e}_{r}-f_{\theta}^{y}\cos\theta\bm{e}_{\theta}+f_{z}^{y}\sin\theta\bm{e}_{z}\big)e^{2\pi iks}\,.

When applied to data of the form (
52
), the single layer operator
\overline{\mathcal{S}}
is again invertible and satisfies

(53)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}[\bm{F}_{x}]

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{-1}{\bm{M}}_{\rm S,n}^{-1}(k)\bm{F}_{x}\,,\qquad\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}[\bm{F}_{y}]=\epsilon^{-1}{\bm{M}}_{\rm S,n}^{-1}(k)\bm{F}_{y}\,,

where
{\bm{M}}_{\rm S,n}^{-1}
is a matrix-valued multiplier of the form

(54)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\bm{M}}_{\rm S,n}^{-1}(k)=m_{\epsilon,{\rm nA}}(k)\,\bm{e}_{r}\otimes\bm{e}_{r}+m_{\epsilon,{\rm nB}}(k)\,(\bm{e}_{r}\otimes\bm{e}_{\theta}+\bm{e}_{\theta}\otimes\bm{e}_{r})+m_{\epsilon,{\rm nD}}(k)\,\bm{e}_{\theta}\otimes\bm{e}_{\theta}

\displaystyle+i\,m_{\epsilon,{\rm nC}}(k)\,(\bm{e}_{r}\otimes\bm{e}_{z}-\bm{e}_{z}\otimes\bm{e}_{r})+i\,m_{\epsilon,{\rm nE}}(k)\,(\bm{e}_{z}\otimes\bm{e}_{\theta}-\bm{e}_{\theta}\otimes\bm{e}_{z})+m_{\epsilon,{\rm nF}}(k)\,\bm{e}_{z}\otimes\bm{e}_{z}\,.

The components of
{\bm{M}}_{\rm S,n}^{-1}(k)
are given explicitly by

(55)

\displaystyle m_{\epsilon,{\rm n}j}(k)

\displaystyle=\frac{Q_{{\rm n}j}(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)}{Q_{\rm nG}(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)}\,,\quad j={\rm A,B,C,D,E,F}\,,

\displaystyle Q_{\rm nA}(z)

\displaystyle=\textstyle\frac{1}{I_{1}K_{1}}\left(4+\frac{4}{z^{2}}\frac{I_{1}}{I_{0}}\frac{K_{1}}{K_{0}}+\frac{2}{z}\left(\frac{K_{1}}{K_{0}}-\frac{I_{1}}{I_{0}}\right)-2z\left(\frac{I_{0}}{I_{1}}-\frac{K_{0}}{K_{1}}\right)-2\left(\frac{I_{0}}{I_{1}}\frac{K_{1}}{K_{0}}+\frac{I_{1}}{I_{0}}\frac{K_{0}}{K_{1}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle Q_{\rm nB}(z)

\displaystyle=\textstyle\frac{1}{I_{1}K_{1}}\left(2\frac{K_{1}}{K_{0}}\left(\frac{I_{0}}{I_{1}}-\frac{I_{1}}{I_{0}}\right)+2\left(\frac{I_{1}}{I_{0}}\frac{K_{0}}{K_{1}}-1\right)+\frac{2}{z}\left(\frac{I_{1}}{I_{0}}-\frac{K_{1}}{K_{0}}\right)-\frac{4}{z^{2}}\frac{I_{1}}{I_{0}}\frac{K_{1}}{K_{0}}\right)

\displaystyle Q_{\rm nC}(z)

\displaystyle=\textstyle\frac{2}{I_{1}K_{1}}\left(\frac{2}{z}\frac{I_{1}}{I_{0}}\frac{K_{1}}{K_{0}}+2\left(\frac{I_{0}}{I_{1}}-\frac{K_{0}}{K_{1}}\right)+\left(\frac{I_{1}}{I_{0}}-\frac{K_{1}}{K_{0}}\right)+z\frac{I_{0}}{I_{1}}\frac{K_{0}}{K_{1}}-z-\frac{4}{z}\right)

\displaystyle Q_{\rm nD}(z)

\displaystyle=\textstyle\frac{1}{I_{1}K_{1}}\bigg(\frac{4}{z^{2}}\frac{I_{1}}{I_{0}}\frac{K_{1}}{K_{0}}+\frac{2}{z}\left(\frac{K_{1}}{K_{0}}-\frac{I_{1}}{I_{0}}\right)+2\left(2\frac{I_{1}}{I_{0}}\frac{K_{1}}{K_{0}}-\frac{I_{1}}{I_{0}}\frac{K_{0}}{K_{1}}-\frac{I_{0}}{I_{1}}\frac{K_{1}}{K_{0}}\right)

\displaystyle\textstyle\displaystyle+z^{2}\left(\frac{I_{0}}{I_{1}}-\frac{I_{1}}{I_{0}}\right)\left(\frac{K_{0}}{K_{1}}-\frac{K_{1}}{K_{0}}\right)\bigg)

\displaystyle Q_{\rm nE}(z)

\displaystyle=\textstyle\frac{1}{I_{1}K_{1}}\left(\frac{4}{z}\frac{I_{1}}{I_{0}}\frac{K_{1}}{K_{0}}-\frac{4}{z}+2\left(\frac{I_{0}}{I_{1}}-\frac{K_{0}}{K_{1}}\right)+z\left(\frac{I_{0}}{I_{1}}-\frac{I_{1}}{I_{0}}\right)\left(\frac{K_{0}}{K_{1}}-\frac{K_{1}}{K_{0}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle Q_{\rm nF}(z)

\displaystyle=\textstyle\frac{1}{I_{1}K_{1}}\bigg(\frac{12}{z^{2}}+\frac{6}{z}\left(\frac{K_{0}}{K_{1}}-\frac{I_{0}}{I_{1}}+\frac{K_{1}}{K_{0}}-\frac{I_{1}}{I_{0}}\right)-3\left(\frac{I_{0}}{I_{1}}\left(\frac{K_{0}}{K_{1}}+\frac{K_{1}}{K_{0}}\right)+\frac{I_{1}}{I_{0}}\frac{K_{0}}{K_{1}}\right)

\displaystyle\textstyle\displaystyle+\left(\frac{I_{1}}{I_{0}}\frac{K_{1}}{K_{0}}+8\right)+2z\left(\frac{K_{0}}{K_{1}}-\frac{I_{0}}{I_{1}}\right)\bigg)

\displaystyle Q_{\rm nG}(z)

\displaystyle=\textstyle\left(\frac{2}{z}+\left(1-z\frac{I_{0}}{I_{1}}\right)\left(\frac{I_{0}}{I_{1}}-\frac{I_{1}}{I_{0}}\right)\right)\left(\frac{2}{z}-\left(1+z\frac{K_{0}}{K_{1}}\right)\left(\frac{K_{0}}{K_{1}}-\frac{K_{1}}{K_{0}}\right)\right)\,.

The calculation of the explicit symbols in Lemma
1.7
appears in Appendix
B
.
For data of the form
\bm{F}_{z}
(
48
) or
\bm{F}_{x},\bm{F}_{y}
(
52
), instead of working with the full inverse single layer operator
\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}
, it will be convenient to define the following angle-averaged inverse single layer along
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
:

(56)

\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\epsilon}[\bm{F}_{j}]:=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}[\bm{F}_{j}]\,\epsilon\,d\theta\,.

Using that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\bm{e}_{z}\,d\theta

\displaystyle=2\pi\bm{e}_{z}\,,\quad\int_{0}^{2\pi}\bm{e}_{r}\,d\theta=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\bm{e}_{\theta}\,d\theta=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\cos\theta\bm{e}_{z}\,d\theta=0\,,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\cos\theta\bm{e}_{r}\,d\theta

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{2\pi}\sin\theta\bm{e}_{\theta}\,d\theta=\pi\bm{e}_{x}\,,\qquad\int_{0}^{2\pi}\sin\theta\bm{e}_{r}\,d\theta=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\cos\theta\bm{e}_{\theta}\,d\theta=\pi\bm{e}_{y}\,,

we immediately obtain the following corollary to Lemma
1.7
.

Corollary 1.8

(Angle-averaged single layer)
.

When applied to functions
\bm{F}_{\mu}
of the form (
48
) or (
52
), the angle-averaged inverse single layer operator
\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\epsilon}
given by (
56
) satisfies

(57)

\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\epsilon}[\bm{F}_{\mu}]=\pi\big(m_{\epsilon,r}^{\mu}(k)f_{r}^{\mu}+m_{\epsilon,\theta}^{\mu}(k)f_{\theta}^{\mu}+m_{\epsilon,z}^{\mu}(k)f_{z}^{\mu}\big)\,e^{2\pi iks}\bm{e}_{\mu}\,,\qquad\mu=x,y,z\,,

where the tangential multipliers
m_{\epsilon,\ell}^{z}
are given by

(58)

m_{\epsilon,z}^{z}(k)=2\,m_{\epsilon,{\rm tA}}(k)\,,\qquad m_{\epsilon,\theta}^{z}(k)=0\,,\qquad m_{\epsilon,r}^{z}(k)=-2i\,m_{\epsilon,{\rm tB}}(k)\,;

and the normal direction multipliers
m_{\epsilon,\ell}^{x}=m_{\epsilon,\ell}^{y}
are given by

(59)

\displaystyle m_{\epsilon,r}^{x}(k)

\displaystyle=m_{\epsilon,r}^{y}(k)=m_{\epsilon,{\rm nA}}(k)-m_{\epsilon,{\rm nB}}(k)\,,

\displaystyle m_{\epsilon,\theta}^{x}(k)

\displaystyle=m_{\epsilon,\theta}^{y}(k)=m_{\epsilon,{\rm nB}}(k)-m_{\epsilon,{\rm nD}}(k)\,,

\displaystyle m_{\epsilon,z}^{x}(k)

\displaystyle=m_{\epsilon,z}^{y}(k)=i\,(m_{\epsilon,{\rm nC}}(k)+m_{\epsilon,{\rm nE}}(k))\,.

It is ultimately the angle-averaged operator
\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\epsilon}
for which we will need detailed information. Before we turn to the mapping properties of
\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\epsilon}
, we may perform a sanity check to demonstrate that we indeed have the correct expressions for the single and double layer multipliers on
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
.
In particular, we can verify that the double and single layer expressions of Lemma
1.7
combine to yield the multipliers
m_{\epsilon,{\rm t}}^{-1}
and
m_{\epsilon,{\rm n}}^{-1}
given by (
23
) and (
24
).
For the tangential direction, we may combine (
47
) and (
58
) to obtain

(60)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}[e^{2\pi iks}\bm{e}_{z}]

\displaystyle=\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\epsilon}\big[\textstyle(\frac{1}{2}{\bf I}-\overline{\mathcal{D}})[e^{2\pi iks}\bm{e}_{z}]\big]

\displaystyle=2\pi\big(m_{\epsilon,{\rm tA}}(k)Q_{\rm tE}(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)+m_{\epsilon,{\rm tB}}(k)Q_{\rm tF}(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)\big)\,e^{2\pi iks}\bm{e}_{z}\,.

For the normal direction, we may combine (
51
) and (
59
) to obtain

(61)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}[e^{2\pi iks}\bm{e}_{x}]

\displaystyle=\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\epsilon}\big[\textstyle(\frac{1}{2}{\bf I}-\overline{\mathcal{D}})[e^{2\pi iks}\bm{e}_{x}]\big]

\displaystyle=\pi\bigg(\big(m_{\epsilon,{\rm nA}}(k)-m_{\epsilon,{\rm nB}}(k)\big)Q_{\rm nN}(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)-\big(m_{\epsilon,{\rm nB}}(k)-m_{\epsilon,{\rm nD}}(k)\big)Q_{\rm nO}(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)

\displaystyle-\big(m_{\epsilon,{\rm nC}}(k)+m_{\epsilon,{\rm nE}}(k)\big)Q_{\rm nP}(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)\bigg)\,e^{2\pi iks}\bm{e}_{x}\,.

The
\bm{e}_{y}
direction is identical. Here each of the
m_{\epsilon,j}
and
Q_{j}
are as in Lemma
1.7
. Just by looking at the components of the expressions (
60
) and (
61
), it is not clear that these are the same as the multipliers
m_{\epsilon,{\rm t}}^{-1}(k)
and
m_{\epsilon,{\rm n}}^{-1}(k)
of Proposition
1.3
. However, by plotting (see figure
2
), we can verify that (
60
) and (
61
) are in fact the same as (
23
) and (
24
) from Proposition
1.3
.

Figure 2
.
Plot of the eigenvalue expressions in directions tangential (left) and normal (right) to the straight filament
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
. Here the horizontal axis is
z=2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert
. In both plots, the solid blue curves (“Direct”) are given by the expressions in Proposition
1.3
while the dotted curves (“BI”) are the boundary integral expressions (
60
) and (
61
), respectively.

The important takeaway from the explicit expressions in Lemma
1.7
is that we can then directly calculate the mapping properties of the angle-averaged operator
\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\epsilon}
when applied to functions consisting of zero- and one-modes in
\theta
.
This is in direct analogy to the Laplace setting
[
59
]
, but the calculation here is much more involved.

For a general vector-valued function
\bm{g}(s,\theta)
defined along the straight cylinder
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
, we write

\displaystyle\bm{g}(s,\theta)=\sum_{j\in\{r,\theta,z\}}\bigg(g_{0}^{j}(s)+\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}g^{j}_{{\rm c},\ell}(s)\cos(\ell\theta)+g^{j}_{\rm{s},\ell}(s)\sin(\ell\theta)\bigg)\bm{e}_{j}\,.

We define the projection
\mathbb{P}_{01}
onto zero- and one-modes in
\theta
by

(62)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{01}\bm{g}

\displaystyle=g_{0}^{z}\,\bm{e}_{z}+g_{0}^{r}\,\bm{e}_{r}+g_{{\rm c},1}^{r}\cos\theta\bm{e}_{r}+g_{{\rm s},1}^{\theta}\sin\theta\bm{e}_{\theta}+g_{{\rm c},1}^{z}\cos\theta\bm{e}_{z}

\displaystyle+g_{{\rm s},1}^{r}\sin\theta\bm{e}_{r}+g_{{\rm c},1}^{\theta}\cos\theta\bm{e}_{\theta}+g_{{\rm s},1}^{z}\sin\theta\bm{e}_{z}\,.

Lemma 1.9
.

Let
\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\epsilon}
be the angle-averaged inverse single layer operator about
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
, given by (
56
). Given
\bm{g}\in C^{1,\alpha}_{s}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})
and the projection operator (
62
), we may decompose
\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\epsilon}\mathbb{P}_{01}
as

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\epsilon}[\mathbb{P}_{01}\bm{g}]=\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\epsilon}^{\epsilon}[\bm{g}]+\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\epsilon}^{+}[\bm{g}]

where

(63)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\epsilon}^{\epsilon}[\bm{g}]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq c\left\lVert\bm{g}\right\rVert_{C_{s}^{0,\alpha}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}+c\,\epsilon\left\lvert\bm{g}\right\rvert_{\dot{C}_{s}^{1,\alpha}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}\,,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\epsilon}^{+}[\bm{g}]\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq c\,\epsilon^{-1}\left\lVert\bm{g}\right\rVert_{C_{s}^{1,\alpha}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}\,.

The proof of Lemma
1.9
appears in section
3.4
.

1.2.6.
Decomposition of SB DtN

Given a curved filament
\Sigma_{\epsilon}
with centerline
\bm{X}(s)\in C^{2,\beta}(\mathbb{T})
, we consider the slender body Dirichlet-to-Neumann map
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
along
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
and aim to extract the corresponding map
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
about the straight filament
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
as the leading order behavior.

Recalling the definition (
27
) of the operator
\Phi
identifying the normal/tangential directions along the curved and straight filaments, we begin by decomposing the boundary integral representation (
44
) as

(64)

\textstyle(\frac{1}{2}{\bf I}-\overline{\mathcal{D}})[\Phi^{-1}\bm{v}(s)]-\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{D}}[\bm{v}(s)]=\overline{\mathcal{S}}[\Phi^{-1}\bm{w}_{0}^{\Phi}]+\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}}[\bm{w}_{0}^{\Phi}]+\Phi^{-1}\mathcal{S}\displaystyle\bigg[\Phi\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Phi^{-1}\bm{w}(s,\theta)\,ds\bigg]\,,

where
\bm{w}_{0}^{\Phi}(s,\theta)
is as in (
28
) and we define

(65)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{D}}[\bm{v}(s)]

\displaystyle:=\Phi^{-1}\mathcal{D}[\bm{v}(s)]-\overline{\mathcal{D}}[\Phi^{-1}\bm{v}(s)]\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}}[\bm{w}_{0}^{\Phi}(s,\theta)]

\displaystyle:=\Phi^{-1}\mathcal{S}[\bm{w}_{0}^{\Phi}(s,\theta)]-\overline{\mathcal{S}}[\Phi^{-1}\bm{w}_{0}^{\Phi}(s,\theta)]\,.

We next would like to use Lemma
1.7
to apply
\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}
to (
64
), but in order to do so we must first apply the projection operator
\mathbb{P}_{01}
(
62
) onto zero- and one-modes in
\theta
.
Note that
\mathbb{P}_{01}
commutes with the straight single and double layer operators
\overline{\mathcal{S}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
about
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
. In particular, applying
\mathbb{P}_{01}
to (
64
), we have

\displaystyle\textstyle(\frac{1}{2}{\bf I}-\overline{\mathcal{D}})[\Phi^{-1}\bm{v}(s)]-\mathbb{P}_{01}\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{D}}[\bm{v}(s)]

\displaystyle=\overline{\mathcal{S}}[\mathbb{P}_{01}\Phi^{-1}\bm{w}_{0}^{\Phi}]+\mathbb{P}_{01}\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}}[\bm{w}_{0}^{\Phi}]+\mathbb{P}_{01}\Phi^{-1}\mathcal{S}\displaystyle\bigg[\Phi\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Phi^{-1}\bm{w}(s,\theta)\,ds\bigg]\,.

We may now apply
\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}
to the above equation, multiply by
\epsilon
, and integrate in
\theta
to obtain

\displaystyle\textstyle\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\epsilon}\big[(\frac{1}{2}{\bf I}-\overline{\mathcal{D}})[\Phi^{-1}\bm{v}(s)]\big]

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathbb{P}_{01}(\Phi^{-1}\bm{w}_{0}^{\Phi})\,\epsilon\,d\theta+\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\epsilon}\big[\mathbb{P}_{01}\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{D}}[\bm{v}]\big]+\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\epsilon}\big[\mathbb{P}_{01}\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}}[\bm{w}_{0}^{\Phi}]\big]

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathcal{A}_{\epsilon}\bigg[\mathbb{P}_{01}\Phi^{-1}\mathcal{S}\displaystyle\bigg[\Phi\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Phi^{-1}\bm{w}(s,\theta)\,ds\bigg]\bigg]

\displaystyle=\Phi^{-1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\bm{w}(s,\theta)\,\epsilon\,d\theta-\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Phi^{-1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\bm{w}(s,\theta)\,\epsilon\,d\theta ds+\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\epsilon}\big[\mathbb{P}_{01}\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{D}}[\bm{v}]\big]

\displaystyle\quad+\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\epsilon}\big[\mathbb{P}_{01}\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}}[\bm{w}_{0}^{\Phi}]\big]+\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\epsilon}\bigg[\mathbb{P}_{01}\Phi^{-1}\mathcal{S}\displaystyle\bigg[\Phi\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Phi^{-1}\bm{w}(s,\theta)\,ds\bigg]\bigg]

\displaystyle=(\Phi^{-1}\bm{f})(s)+\Phi^{-1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\bm{w}(s,\theta)\,\epsilon^{2}\widehat{\kappa}\,d\theta-\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Phi^{-1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\bm{w}(s,\theta)\,\epsilon\,d\theta ds

\displaystyle\quad+\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\epsilon}\big[\mathbb{P}_{01}\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{D}}[\bm{v}]\big]+\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\epsilon}\big[\mathbb{P}_{01}\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}}[\bm{w}_{0}^{\Phi}]\big]+\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\epsilon}\bigg[\mathbb{P}_{01}\Phi^{-1}\mathcal{S}\displaystyle\bigg[\Phi\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Phi^{-1}\bm{w}(s,\theta)\,ds\bigg]\bigg]\,.

Here we have used the expression for
\bm{f}(s)
from (
44
).
The left hand side is exactly
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}[\Phi^{-1}\bm{v}(s)]
, and using that
\bm{f}(s)=\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}^{-1}[\bm{v}(s)]
, we thus obtain the following decomposition of the slender body DtN map:

(66)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}^{-1}[\bm{v}(s)]

\displaystyle=\Phi\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}[\Phi^{-1}\bm{v}(s)]-\int_{0}^{2\pi}\bm{w}(s,\theta)\,\epsilon^{2}\widehat{\kappa}\,d\theta+\Phi\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Phi^{-1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\bm{w}(s,\theta)\,\epsilon\,d\theta ds

\displaystyle-\Phi\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\epsilon}\big[\mathbb{P}_{01}\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{D}}[\bm{v}]\big]-\Phi\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\epsilon}\big[\mathbb{P}_{01}\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}}[\bm{w}_{0}^{\Phi}]\big]-\Phi\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\epsilon}\bigg[\mathbb{P}_{01}\Phi^{-1}\mathcal{S}\displaystyle\bigg[\Phi\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Phi^{-1}\bm{w}(s,\theta)\,ds\bigg]\bigg]\,.

We will rely on this representation of
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
to prove Theorem
1.5
. In particular, we show that the five remainder terms appearing in (
66
) are lower order with respect to regularity or size in
\epsilon
. These terms may each be bounded using a combination of Lemma
1.9
and the following series of key lemmas.

The first two lemmas give the mapping properties of the single and double layer remainders
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{D}}
, respectively.

Lemma 1.10

(Single layer remainder)
.

Let
0<\alpha<\beta<1
and consider a filament
\Sigma_{\epsilon}
with centerline
\bm{X}(s)\in C^{2,\beta}(\mathbb{T})
. Let the single layer remainder
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}}
be as defined in (
65
), and let the map
\Phi
be as in (
27
). Given
\bm{\varphi}\in C^{0,\alpha}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})
with
\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Phi^{-1}\bm{\varphi}(s,\theta)\,ds=0
,
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}}
may be decomposed as

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}}[\bm{\varphi}]=\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S},\epsilon}[\bm{\varphi}]+\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S},+}[\bm{\varphi}]

where

(67)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S},\epsilon}[\bm{\varphi}]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leq c(\kappa_{*,\alpha},c_{\Gamma})\,\epsilon^{2-\alpha}\left\lVert\bm{\varphi}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\,,\quad\left\lvert\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S},\epsilon}[\bm{\varphi}]\right\rvert_{\dot{C}_{s}^{1,\alpha}}\leq c(\kappa_{*,\alpha^{+}},c_{\Gamma})\,\epsilon^{1-\alpha^{+}}\left\lVert\bm{\varphi}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}}

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S},+}[\bm{\varphi}]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leq c(\kappa_{*,\alpha},c_{\Gamma})\,\epsilon^{1-\alpha}\left\lVert\bm{\varphi}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\,,\quad\left\lvert\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S},+}[\bm{\varphi}]\right\rvert_{\dot{C}_{s}^{1,\beta}}\leq c(\kappa_{*,\beta},c_{\Gamma})\,\epsilon^{1-2\beta}\left\lVert\bm{\varphi}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}}

for any
\alpha^{+}\in(\alpha,\beta]
.
Furthermore, given two nearby filaments with centerlines
\bm{X}^{(a)}(s)
,
\bm{X}^{(b)}(s)
satisfying Lemma
1.1
, let
\bm{\varphi}_{0}^{\Phi^{(a)}}=\bm{\varphi}-\int_{\mathbb{T}}(\Phi^{(a)})^{-1}\bm{\varphi}(s,\theta)\,ds
and
\bm{\varphi}_{0}^{\Phi^{(b)}}=\bm{\varphi}-\int_{\mathbb{T}}(\Phi^{(b)})^{-1}\bm{\varphi}(s,\theta)\,ds
. The difference between the corresponding single layer remainders
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}}^{(a)}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}}^{(b)}
may be decomposed as above, and the components satisfy

(68)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S},\epsilon}^{(a)}[\bm{\varphi}_{0}^{\Phi^{(a)}}]-\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S},\epsilon}^{(b)}[\bm{\varphi}_{0}^{\Phi^{(b)}}]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leq c(\kappa_{*,\alpha}^{(a)},\kappa_{*,\alpha}^{(b)},c_{\Gamma})\,\epsilon^{2-\alpha}\left\lVert\bm{X}^{(a)}-\bm{X}^{(b)}\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha}}\left\lVert\bm{\varphi}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S},\epsilon}^{(a)}[\bm{\varphi}_{0}^{\Phi^{(a)}}]-\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S},\epsilon}^{(b)}[\bm{\varphi}_{0}^{\Phi^{(b)}}]\right\rvert_{\dot{C}^{1,\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leq c(\kappa_{*,\alpha^{+}}^{(a)},\kappa_{*,\alpha^{+}}^{(b)},c_{\Gamma})\,\epsilon^{1-\alpha^{+}}\left\lVert\bm{X}^{(a)}-\bm{X}^{(b)}\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha^{+}}}\left\lVert\bm{\varphi}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}}

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S},+}^{(a)}[\bm{\varphi}_{0}^{\Phi^{(a)}}]-\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S},+}^{(b)}[\bm{\varphi}_{0}^{\Phi^{(b)}}]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leq c(\kappa_{*,\alpha}^{(a)},\kappa_{*,\alpha}^{(b)},c_{\Gamma})\,\epsilon^{1-\alpha}\left\lVert\bm{X}^{(a)}-\bm{X}^{(b)}\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha}}\left\lVert\bm{\varphi}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S},+}^{(a)}[\bm{\varphi}_{0}^{\Phi^{(a)}}]-\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S},+}^{(b)}[\bm{\varphi}_{0}^{\Phi^{(b)}}]\right\rvert_{\dot{C}^{1,\beta}}

\displaystyle\leq c(\kappa_{*,\beta}^{(a)},\kappa_{*,\beta}^{(b)},c_{\Gamma})\,\epsilon^{1-2\beta}\left\lVert\bm{X}^{(a)}-\bm{X}^{(b)}\right\rVert_{C^{2,\beta}}\left\lVert\bm{\varphi}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}}\,.

The proof of Lemma
1.10
appears in section
4.2
.

Lemma 1.11

(Double layer remainder)
.

Let
0<\gamma<\beta<1
and consider a filament
\Sigma_{\epsilon}
with centerline
\bm{X}(s)\in C^{2,\beta}(\mathbb{T})
. Let the double layer remainder
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{D}}
be as defined in (
65
). Given
\bm{\psi}\in C^{0,\gamma}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})
, we have that
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{D}}
satisfies

(69)

\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{D}}[\bm{\psi}]\right\rVert_{C^{1,\gamma}}\leq c(\kappa_{*,\gamma^{+}},c_{\Gamma})\,\epsilon^{-\gamma^{+}}\left\lVert\bm{\psi}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\gamma}}

for any
\gamma^{+}\in(\gamma,\beta]
.
Furthermore, given two nearby filaments with centerlines
\bm{X}^{(a)}(s)
,
\bm{X}^{(b)}(s)
satisfying Lemma
1.1
, the difference between their corresponding double layer remainders
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{D}}^{(a)}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{D}}^{(b)}
satisfies

(70)

\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{D}}^{(a)}[\bm{\psi}]-\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{D}}^{(b)}[\bm{\psi}]\right\rVert_{C^{1,\gamma}}\leq c(\kappa_{*,\gamma^{+}}^{(a)},\kappa_{*,\gamma^{+}}^{(b)},c_{\Gamma})\,\epsilon^{-\gamma^{+}}\left\lVert\bm{X}^{(a)}-\bm{X}^{(b)}\right\rVert_{C^{2,\gamma^{+}}}\left\lVert\bm{\psi}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\gamma}}\,.

The proof of Lemma
1.11
appears in section
4.3
. In addition to Lemmas
1.10
and
1.11
, we will require a bound for the single layer operator applied to constant-in-
s
functions along
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
. We show the following.

Lemma 1.12

(Single layer applied to constant-in-
s
)
.

Let
0<\alpha<\beta<1
and consider a filament
\Sigma_{\epsilon}
with centerline
\bm{X}(s)\in C^{2,\beta}(\mathbb{T})
. Given
\overline{\bm{h}}(\theta)\in C^{0,\alpha}(2\pi\mathbb{T})
of the form
\overline{\bm{h}}=\overline{h}_{1}(\theta)\bm{e}_{z}+\overline{h}_{2}(\theta)\bm{e}_{x}+\overline{h}_{3}(\theta)\bm{e}_{y}
, and recalling the map
\Phi
(
27
), we may decompose

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}[\Phi\overline{\bm{h}}(\theta)]=\mathcal{H}_{\epsilon}[\Phi\overline{\bm{h}}(\theta)]+\mathcal{H}_{+}[\Phi\overline{\bm{h}}(\theta)]

where

(71)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{H}_{\epsilon}[\Phi\overline{\bm{h}}]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leq c(\kappa_{*,\alpha},c_{\Gamma})\,\epsilon^{2-\alpha}\left\lVert\overline{\bm{h}}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\,,\quad\left\lvert\mathcal{H}_{\epsilon}[\Phi\overline{\bm{h}}]\right\rvert_{\dot{C}_{s}^{1,\alpha}}\leq c(\kappa_{*,\alpha^{+}},c_{\Gamma})\,\epsilon^{1-\alpha^{+}}\left\lVert\overline{\bm{h}}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}}

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{H}_{+}[\Phi\overline{\bm{h}}]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leq c(\kappa_{*,\alpha},c_{\Gamma})\,\epsilon^{1-\alpha}\left\lVert\overline{\bm{h}}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\,,\quad\left\lvert\mathcal{H}_{+}[\Phi\overline{\bm{h}}]\right\rvert_{\dot{C}_{s}^{1,\beta}}\leq c(\kappa_{*,\beta},c_{\Gamma})\,\epsilon^{1-2\beta}\left\lVert\overline{\bm{h}}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}}

for any
\alpha^{+}\in(\alpha,\beta]
.
In addition, given two nearby curves
\bm{X}^{(a)}
,
\bm{X}^{(b)}
satisfying Lemma
1.1
, the corresponding differences
\mathcal{H}_{\epsilon}^{(a)}-\mathcal{H}_{\epsilon}^{(b)}
and
\mathcal{H}_{+}^{(a)}-\mathcal{H}_{+}^{(b)}
satisfy

(72)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{H}_{\epsilon}^{(a)}[\Phi^{(a)}\overline{\bm{h}}]-\mathcal{H}_{\epsilon}^{(b)}[\Phi^{(b)}\overline{\bm{h}}]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leq c(\kappa_{*,\alpha}^{(a)},\kappa_{*,\alpha}^{(b)},c_{\Gamma})\,\epsilon^{2-\alpha}\left\lVert\bm{X}^{(a)}-\bm{X}^{(b)}\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha}}\left\lVert\overline{\bm{h}}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathcal{H}_{\epsilon}^{(a)}[\Phi^{(a)}\overline{\bm{h}}]-\mathcal{H}_{\epsilon}^{(b)}[\Phi^{(b)}\overline{\bm{h}}]\right\rvert_{\dot{C}_{s}^{1,\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leq c(\kappa_{*,\alpha^{+}}^{(a)},\kappa_{*,\alpha^{+}}^{(b)},c_{\Gamma})\,\epsilon^{1-\alpha^{+}}\left\lVert\bm{X}^{(a)}-\bm{X}^{(b)}\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha^{+}}}\left\lVert\overline{\bm{h}}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}}

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{H}_{+}^{(a)}[\Phi^{(a)}\overline{\bm{h}}]-\mathcal{H}_{+}^{(b)}[\Phi^{(b)}\overline{\bm{h}}]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leq c(\kappa_{*,\alpha}^{(a)},\kappa_{*,\alpha}^{(b)},c_{\Gamma})\,\epsilon^{1-\alpha}\left\lVert\bm{X}^{(a)}-\bm{X}^{(b)}\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha}}\left\lVert\overline{\bm{h}}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathcal{H}_{+}^{(a)}[\Phi^{(a)}\overline{\bm{h}}]-\mathcal{H}_{+}^{(b)}[\Phi^{(b)}\overline{\bm{h}}]\right\rvert_{\dot{C}_{s}^{1,\beta}}

\displaystyle\leq c(\kappa_{*,\beta}^{(a)},\kappa_{*,\beta}^{(b)},c_{\Gamma})\,\epsilon^{1-2\beta}\left\lVert\bm{X}^{(a)}-\bm{X}^{(b)}\right\rVert_{C^{2,\beta}}\left\lVert\overline{\bm{h}}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}}\,.

The proof of Lemma
1.12
appears in section
4.4
.

The next lemma concerns bounds for the full Neumann data
\bm{w}(s,\theta)
given by (
42
) and appearing in the decomposition (
66
).

Lemma 1.13

(Bounds for
\bm{w}(s,\theta)
)
.

Let
0<\alpha<\gamma<\beta<1
and consider a slender body
\Sigma_{\epsilon}
with centerline
\bm{X}(s)\in C^{2,\beta}(\mathbb{T})
.
Given
\theta
-independent Dirichlet data
\bm{v}(s)\in C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})
along the filament surface
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
, let
\bm{u}(\bm{x})
,
\bm{x}\in\Omega_{\epsilon}
, denote the corresponding solution to the Stokes equations in
\Omega_{\epsilon}
, and let
\bm{w}(s,\theta)
denote the corresponding Neumann boundary value
\bm{w}=-\bm{\sigma}[\bm{u}]\bm{n}\big|_{\Gamma_{\epsilon}}
. Then
\bm{w}
may be decomposed as

\displaystyle\bm{w}(s,\theta)=\bm{w}_{\epsilon}(s,\theta)+\bm{w}_{+}(s,\theta)

where

(73)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{w}_{\epsilon}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}

\displaystyle\leq c(\kappa_{*},c_{\Gamma})\left\lVert\bm{v}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{w}_{+}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\gamma}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}

\displaystyle\leq c(\epsilon,\kappa_{*,\gamma^{+}},c_{\Gamma})\,\left\lVert\bm{v}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{T})}

for any
\gamma^{+}\in(\gamma,\beta]
.
In addition, given two nearby filaments
\Sigma_{\epsilon}^{(a)},\Sigma_{\epsilon}^{(b)}
with centerlines
\bm{X}^{(a)}
and
\bm{X}^{(b)}
satisfying Lemma
1.1
, we have

(74)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{w}_{\epsilon}^{(a)}-\bm{w}_{\epsilon}^{(b)}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}

\displaystyle\leq c(\kappa_{*}^{(a)},\kappa_{*}^{(b)},c_{\Gamma})\left\lVert\bm{X}^{(a)}-\bm{X}^{(b)}\right\rVert_{C^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\left\lVert\bm{v}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{w}_{+}^{(a)}-\bm{w}_{+}^{(b)}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\gamma}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}

\displaystyle\leq c(\epsilon,\kappa_{*,\gamma^{+}}^{(a)},\kappa_{*,\gamma^{+}}^{(b)},c_{\Gamma})\,\left\lVert\bm{X}^{(a)}-\bm{X}^{(b)}\right\rVert_{C^{2,\gamma^{+}}(\mathbb{T})}\left\lVert\bm{v}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{T})}\,,

where
\bm{w}^{(a)},\bm{w}^{(b)}
denote the Neumann data along filaments
\Sigma_{\epsilon}^{(a)}
and
\Sigma_{\epsilon}^{(b)}
, respectively.

In both (
73
) and (
74
), the
\epsilon
-dependence is explicit in the bounds for
\bm{w}_{\epsilon}
but not for
\bm{w}_{+}
.
The proof of Lemma
1.13
is given in section
5
.

The final key lemma concerns Hölder space estimates for the slender body NtD map, which will be used in inverting the representation (
66
) of
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
to obtain a decomposition of
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
instead.

Lemma 1.14

(Slender body NtD in Hölder spaces)
.

For
0<\alpha<\alpha^{+}<1
, given a filament
\Sigma_{\epsilon}
with centerline
\bm{X}(s)\in C^{3,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})
and slender body force data
\bm{f}(s)\in C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})
, the slender body NtD map
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}[\bm{f}]
(
12
) satisfies

(75)

\left\lVert\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}[\bm{f}]\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}\leq c(\epsilon,\left\lVert\bm{X}_{ss}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}},c_{\Gamma})\left\lVert\bm{f}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}\,.

In addition, given two nearby filaments with centerlines
\bm{X}^{(a)}(s)
,
\bm{X}^{(b)}(s)
satisfying Lemma
1.1
, the difference between the corresponding NtD maps
(\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}^{(a)}-\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}^{(b)})[\bm{f}]
satisfies

(76)

\left\lVert(\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}^{(a)}-\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}^{(b)})[\bm{f}]\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}\leq c(\epsilon,\|\bm{X}_{ss}^{(a)}\|_{C^{1,\alpha}},\|\bm{X}_{ss}^{(b)}\|_{C^{1,\alpha}},c_{\Gamma})\left\lVert\bm{X}^{(a)}-\bm{X}^{(b)}\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha^{+}}(\mathbb{T})}\left\lVert\bm{f}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}\,.
